Comprehensive Test Failure Analysis
I'll analyze these failures by grouping them into conceptual categories, focusing on diagnosis of the underlying architectural issues rather than quick fixes.
1. The Descriptor Map Bug (Tests 3, 4, 7, and likely 2, 8)
Core Issue: In getProperties(), when populating $propertiesMap$ for primitives and types, the code stores raw descriptors instead of $ObjectiveRep wrappers:
typescript
this[$propertiesOwn$].forEach($descriptor => 
    this[$propertiesMap$]!.set($descriptor.$key!.literal, $descriptor[$literal$]));
    //                                                    ^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^
    // Stores PropertyDescriptor, not $ObjectiveRep!
Why This Matters: When $property() retrieves from the map and calls .$as('property'), it's calling that method on a raw PropertyDescriptor object, which doesn't have the method.
Philosophical Question: This reveals a tension in your architecture - the map is meant as a fast lookup cache, but by storing raw descriptors, you've broken the abstraction boundary. Should $propertiesMap$ store $ObjectiveReps (consistent with the role-based architecture) or is this an intentional optimization that needs different retrieval logic?
Tests Affected:
Test 3: "should work with properties" - direct map lookup failure
Test 4: "should identify method descriptors" - same issue via $method
Test 7: "should handle getter/setter role transformations" - same issue
Test 2 & 8: Likely related cascading failures
Diagnostic Question: Is the map meant to be a cache of the $ObjectiveRep instances, or a separate index? The code suggests you want caching, but the implementation created a semantic mismatch.
2. The typeof/typeof/typeof/parameters Conceptual Mismatch (Tests 9, 10)
Core Issue: $typeof(function) returns a TYPE representation (the Function constructor), not the function instance itself. When you access .$parameters on this, you're asking for the parameters of the Function constructor, not the actual function.
typescript
const compute = (a: number, b: string, ...rest: boolean[]) => {};
const func = $typeof(compute);  // Returns type representation
const parameters = func.$parameters;  // Gets Function constructor's params (none)
The Semantic Conflict:
$typeof(5) → type representation of Number (correct for primitives)
$typeof(func) → type representation of Function (but tests expect the function's OWN metadata)
Philosophical Question: Should $typeof on a function return:
The function AS a type (current: Function constructor) - semantically pure but loses instance metadata
The function AS an instance OF Function - but then how do you access the function's own parameter signature?
Tests' Expectation: The tests expect $typeof(func).$parameters to return the specific function's parameters, suggesting they want instance-level reflection, not type-level.
Diagnostic Question: Does your type system distinguish between:
A function as a TYPE (has prototype, is instantiable)
A function as an INSTANCE of Function (has its own parameter signature)
A function as an OBJECT with properties (has own property descriptors)
The tests suggest you need all three perspectives, but $typeof only provides one.
3. The Getter/Setter Boolean Logic Mystery (Tests 5, 6)
Observable Behavior:
Test 5: Getter-only property shows isWritable === true (should be false)
Test 6: Setter-only property shows isReadable === true (should be false)
The Code Looks Correct:
typescript
get isReadable(): boolean {
    const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
    return 'value' in descriptor || 'get' in descriptor;
}

get isWritable(): boolean {
    const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
    return ('value' in descriptor && descriptor.writable!) || 'set' in descriptor;
}
Diagnostic Hypothesis: The descriptor itself might not be what you expect. Possibilities:
Descriptor corruption: If this[$literal$] is undefined or not a proper PropertyDescriptor, the 'value' in descriptor check might throw or return unexpected results
Prototype contamination: If descriptors are being shared or inherited incorrectly
Test object creation: The test objects { get readOnly() {...} } might not be creating the descriptors you expect
Critical Test to Run: What does Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor({ get readOnly() { return 42; } }, 'readOnly') actually return? Does it have ONLY get, or does it have other fields?
Diagnostic Question: Are these properties being accessed on the RIGHT $ObjectiveRep? Is the test accessing a property rep that has the correct role and literal?
4. The Inherited Property Undefined Value (Test 1)
Issue: instance.$property('toString', 'all').$value.literal returns undefined instead of Object.prototype.toString.
Current Logic Path:
typescript
$property(name, 'all') 
→ this.$type.$property('all').$of(this)  // if not found in own
→ delegates to type
→ type gets property from prototype
→ creates new $ObjectiveRep via $of(instance)
Potential Breaks:
The $of chain: When you call $of(instance) on a property from the type, you create a new $ObjectiveRep with Object.create(). This new object might not have [$value$] cached, and when you access .$value, it tries to create it from this[$literal$], but the literal might not be transferred correctly.
The recursive 'all' call: When you call this.$type.$property('all'), does the type properly recursively search ITS type's properties? Or does it only look at its own prototype?
Philosophical Issue: You're trying to represent "this property, which is defined on the type, but accessed from an instance". The $of mechanism is meant to handle this, but there's a semantic mismatch between:
The property's descriptor (from the prototype)
The property's "ownership" (instance vs type vs prototype)
The property's value (which might need different evaluation context)
Diagnostic Question: Should properties found via 'all' maintain their original descriptor, or should they be re-evaluated in the context of the instance?
5. Symbol Properties Return Undefined (Test 2)
Issue: Symbol-keyed properties aren't being found or returned correctly.
Likely Cause: When storing in $propertiesMap$, symbol keys work fine. But when retrieving:
typescript
this[$propertiesMap$]!.get(name)
If name is a Symbol, the Map lookup should work. But if there's type coercion or string conversion happening somewhere in the chain, symbols could be lost.
Diagnostic Questions:
Are symbols making it into the map correctly?
Is $descriptor.$key!.literal returning the Symbol, or a string representation?
When you call $property(sym), is the symbol being passed through unchanged?
Overall Architectural Observations
1. The Three-Layer Problem
Your system has three intermingled concerns:
Property descriptors (JavaScript runtime reality)
$ObjectiveRep wrappers (your semantic representation)
Role transformations (your type system)
The bugs suggest these layers aren't cleanly separated. Maps store layer 1, but consumers expect layer 2.
2. The $of Propagation Question
When you transform a property "of" a type to be "of" an instance via $of(), you're creating a new object via Object.create(). But:
Does it need new caches?
Does it need a new literal?
Should it re-evaluate getters like $value?
The current code assumes prototype inheritance solves this, but the test failures suggest it doesn't.
3. The Function Representation Duality
Functions are both:
Types (have prototypes, can construct)
Instances (have specific parameter signatures)
Your $typeof currently collapses these into one representation, but tests expect both.
Recommendations for Test Validity
Tests that might be invalid (expecting behavior that conflicts with your semantic model):
Tests 9, 10: If $typeof is meant to return TYPE-level representation, these tests are wrong
Tests that reveal real bugs:
Tests 3, 4, 7: Clear implementation bug in map storage
Test 1: Inherited property resolution is broken
Tests 5, 6: Either bug or test misunderstanding
Tests that need investigation:
Test 2, 8: Could be cascading failures from other bugs
Would you like me to dive deeper into any specific category?

Claude
17:14 UTC, 8 November 2025
You've got a semantic issue with isField and how it interacts with writability.
The Problem
Your current logic:
typescript
get isWritable(): boolean {
    return this.isField || !!descriptor.set;
}
This says "all fields are writable OR it has a setter". But frozen fields are fields that are NOT writable. Example:
javascript
const obj = {};
Object.defineProperty(obj, 'frozen', { 
    value: 42, 
    writable: false  // This is a field, but NOT writable!
});
JavaScript Descriptor Semantics
There are two types of descriptors:
Data/Value descriptor: Has value and writable
Can be read if it exists (always readable)
Can be written only if writable === true
Accessor descriptor: Has get and/or set (no writable field)
Can be read if get exists
Can be written if set exists
The Fix
typescript
get isReadable(): boolean {
    const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
    return 'value' in descriptor || !!descriptor.get;
    // Value props are always readable, accessor props need a getter
}

get isWritable(): boolean {
    const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
    return ('value' in descriptor && descriptor.writable!) || !!descriptor.set;
    // Value props need writable:true, accessor props need a setter
}
Your original version had this right! The issue isn't with the boolean logic, it's elsewhere.
To Answer Your Direct Question
Does writable cover that it is a field or has a getter and is it frozen?
No. writable is:
ONLY present on value descriptors (fields)
ABSENT on accessor descriptors (getters/setters)
Can be false (frozen field) or true (mutable field)
Or could a property with only a setter be marked as writable?
An accessor property with only a setter:
Does NOT have a writable field (that field doesn't exist for accessors)
SHOULD be considered writable (you can write via obj.prop = val)
Has set function present, get absent
So you need: return !!descriptor.writable || !!descriptor.set
But wait - descriptor.writable could be undefined for accessors, so you need:
typescript
return ('value' in descriptor && descriptor.writable!) || !!descriptor.set
This checks "is it a value prop AND writable, OR does it have a setter?"
Doug

get isWritable(): boolean {
        const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
        return !!descriptor.writable || !!descriptor.get;
    }
Claude
17:16 UTC, 8 November 2025
No! You've got getters and setters backwards.
The Semantics
get = readable (you can GET the value)
set = writable (you can SET the value)
javascript
const obj = {
  get readOnly() { return 42; }
};

obj.readOnly;      // ✓ Works - can READ
obj.readOnly = 5;  // ✗ Fails - cannot WRITE (no setter!)
javascript
const obj = {
  set writeOnly(v) { console.log(v); }
};

obj.writeOnly = 5;  // ✓ Works - can WRITE  
obj.writeOnly;      // ✗ Returns undefined - cannot READ (no getter!)
The Correct Logic
typescript
get isReadable(): boolean {
    const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
    return 'value' in descriptor || !!descriptor.get;
    // Fields are always readable, accessors need a getter
}

get isWritable(): boolean {
    const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
    return ('value' in descriptor && descriptor.writable!) || !!descriptor.set;
    // Fields need writable:true, accessors need a setter
}
Think of it this way:
GETter makes it readABLE
SETter makes it writABLE

17:17 UTC, 8 November 2025
Almost, but there's a critical issue with 0.
The Problem
Your isField is defined as:
typescript
get isField(): boolean {
    return !this.isProperty && !this.isMethod;
}
So methods are NOT fields. That means:
typescript
return this.isField || !!descriptor.get;
For a method:
this.isField = false (because isMethod = true)
!!descriptor.get = false (methods use value, not get)
Result: methods appear unreadable! ❌
The Fix
You need to check if the descriptor has a value (which covers both fields AND methods):
typescript
get isReadable(): boolean {
    const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
    return 'value' in descriptor || !!descriptor.get;
}

get isWritable(): boolean {
    const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
    return ('value' in descriptor && descriptor.writable!) || !!descriptor.set;
}
Why This Matters
Data descriptors (fields + methods): have value, readable always, writable only if writable: true
Accessor descriptors (properties): have get/set, readable if get exists, writable if set exists
Your isField is a semantic distinction (non-function value), but isReadable/isWritable need the descriptor-level distinction (data vs accessor).

17:49 UTC, 8 November 2025
Comprehensive Analysis Round 2
1. The Stack Overflow: Infinite Recursion in $property (Tests 1, 3-7)
The Smoking Gun:
typescript
$property(name: string | symbol, whos: 'own' | 'all' = 'all'): $ObjectiveRep {
    this.getProperties(whos);
    const $undefined = $instanceof(undefined).$as('property').$of(this);
    const $property = this[$propertiesMap$]!.get(name);
    if ($property) return $property;
    if (whos === 'own') return $property || $undefined;
    return this.$type.literal ? this.$type.$property(whos).$of(this) : $undefined;
    //                                              ^^^^^ STILL 'all'!
}
The Infinite Loop:
Instance calls $property('toString', 'all')
Not found in own properties
Delegates to type: this.$type.$property('all') ← still passing 'all'!
Type doesn't find it in own properties
Type delegates to ITS type: this.$type.$property('all') ← still 'all'!
Repeat until stack overflow
The Fix Pattern: When delegating to the type, you should search only the type's OWN properties:
typescript
return this.$type.literal ? this.$type.$property(name, 'own').$of(this) : $undefined;
//                                              Add name here ^^^^
Philosophical Issue: The recursion represents a fundamental question about property inheritance. Should $property(name, 'all') mean:
"Find this property, checking inheritance" (current broken behavior)
"Find this property in MY chain, where each level only checks its own" (what it should be)
The second interpretation breaks the recursion: each level only searches 'own', and the 'all' traversal happens via the explicit prototype chain walk in getProperties(), not via recursive $property calls.
2. The Map Storage Bug: Still Storing Raw Descriptors (Underlying cause for many issues)
Line 375 and 380:
typescript
this[$propertiesMap$]!.set($descriptor.$key!.literal.property, $descriptor[$literal$]);
//                                                   ^^^^^^^^ WHAT?  ^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^ Raw descriptor!
Two Bugs Here:
Wrong value stored: $descriptor[$literal$] is the raw PropertyDescriptor, not the $ObjectiveRep wrapper
Wrong key used: $descriptor.$key!.literal.property is trying to access .property on a string
What $key.literal Actually Is: Looking at the $key getter:
typescript
this[$key$] = new $ObjectiveRep('identifier', this, this[$literal$].property, ...);
//                                                    ^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^
The third argument (literal) is this[$literal$].property, which is the property NAME (string/symbol). So $key.literal IS the property name, and you shouldn't append .property to it.
The Correct Line Should Be:
typescript
this[$propertiesMap$]!.set($descriptor.$key!.literal, $descriptor);
//                                        remove ^^^^^^^^  ^^^^^^^^^^ Store the wrapper, not the literal!
This same bug exists on lines 375 and 380.
3. The isMethod Philosophy Crisis (Tests 2, 8-12)
Current Implementation:
typescript
get isMethod(): boolean {
    return this.$value.$type.$is('function') && this.$value.form === 'method';
    //                                                      ^^^^^^^^^^^^^^
    //                                          Checks SYNTACTIC form
}
What the Tests Expect:
typescript
const api = {
    field: 'text',
    process: function() { return 1; },  // form = 'function'
    compute: () => 2,                   // form = 'lambda'
    transform(x: number) { return x * 3; }  // form = 'method'
};
expect(instance.$methods().length).toBe(3);  // Expects ALL three!
The Philosophical Conflict:
Syntactic Definition (current code):
A method is ONLY a function defined using method syntax
transform() {} is a method
compute: () => {} is NOT a method (it's a lambda stored as a field)
Semantic/Runtime Definition (test expectation):
A method is ANY function value stored in a property
All function values are methods
The syntactic form doesn't matter at runtime
The Runtime JavaScript Truth: At runtime, there's no difference between these:
javascript
{ method() {} }
{ method: function() {} }
{ method: () => {} }
They're all just properties with function values. The syntax is gone.
Diagnostic Question: Does Doug want to preserve syntactic distinctions at runtime (philosophically interesting but pragmatically limiting), or use semantic runtime classifications (matches JavaScript's actual behavior)?
If Semantic (my guess based on runtime reflection philosophy):
typescript
get isMethod(): boolean {
    const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
    return 'value' in descriptor && typeof descriptor.value === 'function';
}
But Then isField Needs Fixing Too:
typescript
get isField(): boolean {
    const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
    return 'value' in descriptor && typeof descriptor.value !== 'function';
}
This creates a clean trichotomy:
Fields: data properties with non-function values
Methods: data properties with function values
Properties: accessor properties (get/set)
4. Cascading Failures from isMethod
Test 8: "should filter only fields" - expects 2, gets 4
Because isField is defined as !this.isProperty && !this.isMethod
But isMethod is only returning true for method-syntax functions
So arrow functions and function expressions are incorrectly classified as fields
Test 10: "should filter only methods" - expects 3, gets 1
Only the method-syntax function passes the form === 'method' check
Test 11: "should find methods on class prototypes" - missing 'constructor'
The constructor probably has form === 'class' or form === 'function', not form === 'method'
Test 12: "should handle bound methods" - .literal() fails
The test creates { boundMethod: bound } where bound is a bound function
bound is treated as a field (not a method) due to broken isMethod
So $method('boundMethod') returns the undefined sentinel
The undefined sentinel's $value.literal is undefined, hence the error

// Line 212
return this.$type.literal ? this.$type.$property(name, 'own').$of(this) : $undefined;

typescript
// Lines 375 and 380
this[$propertiesMap$]!.set($descriptor.$key!.literal, $descriptor);
//                          remove .property ^^^^^^    ^^^^^^^^^^^^ Store wrapper
Fix 3: Use semantic definition of isMethod
typescript
get isMethod(): boolean {
    const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
    return 'value' in descriptor && typeof descriptor.value === 'function';
}

get isField(): boolean {
    const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
    return 'value' in descriptor && typeof descriptor.value !== 'function';
}
These three fixes should resolve all 13 failures. The core issue is the tension between syntactic preservation (what the code tried to do) vs runtime semantics (what JavaScript actually has and what tests expect).

18:56 UTC, 8 November 2025
You're right to challenge me. Let me trace through what $of actually does:
What $of Really Does
typescript
$of(of: $ObjectiveRep | $ObjectiveRole): $ObjectiveRep {
    if (this[$of$] == of) return this;
    const $this = this.$new();              // Object.create(this)
    $this[$of$] = of;                       // Override the [$of$] field
    const $$this = this[$roles$].get($this.$role.$ref);  // Check cache
    return $$this ? $$this : $this;
}
$of creates a derived object via prototypal inheritance that:
Inherits everything from the original via Object.create(this)
Overrides only the 0 field to change the context
Caches/retrieves this view in the roles map
So when you do:
typescript
type.$property('toString', 'own').$of(instance)
You get a property representation that:
Has all the same data (descriptor, value, etc.) from the type's version
But reports being "of" the instance instead of "of" the type
The $role.$ref will be different because it incorporates the new [$of$]
But Wait - There's Another Bug I Missed!
Looking at line 212 more carefully:
typescript
return this.$type.literal ? this.$type.$property(whos).$of(this) : $undefined;
//                                              ^^^^^ MISSING name parameter!
This should be:
typescript
return this.$type.literal ? this.$type.$property(name, whos).$of(this) : $undefined;
//                                              ^^^^^ ADD name!
Currently it's calling $property(whos) which means:
First arg (name) = 'all' ← looking for property NAMED 'all'!
Second arg defaults to 'all'
So it's trying to find a property called "all", not finding it, recursing forever.
So My Fix Should Be
typescript
return this.$type.literal ? this.$type.$property(name, 'own').$of(this) : $undefined;
//                                              ^^^^  ^^^^
//                                              Add name, change to 'own'
Is my understanding of 0 affecting this fix?
I don't think so. The $of operation is separate from the recursion fix. We need:
Pass name (so we're looking for the RIGHT property)
Pass 'own' (so each level only searches its own, preventing infinite recursion)
Then $of(this) recontextualizes it to belong to the instance
Am I missing something about how 0 works that would make this wrong?

19:02 UTC, 8 November 2025
You're absolutely right. Arrow functions lexically capture this - they can't be real methods because they can't dynamically bind to the instance. This IS a semantically important distinction.
The Proper Classification
typescript
get isMethod(): boolean {
    const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
    if (!('value' in descriptor)) return false;
    if (typeof descriptor.value !== 'function') return false;
    
    const form = this.$value.form;
    // Methods and functions can bind 'this', arrows cannot
    return form === 'method' || form === 'function';
}

get isField(): boolean {
    const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
    if (!('value' in descriptor)) return false;
    
    // Fields are non-functions OR arrow functions (function-like fields)
    if (typeof descriptor.value !== 'function') return true;
    
    // Arrow functions are lexically scoped, more like fields than methods
    return this.$value.form === 'lambda';
}
The Semantic Hierarchy
Data Descriptors (have value):
Fields: Non-function values + arrow functions
field: 'text'
compute: () => 2 ← functionally a field (can't bind this)
Methods: Functions that can bind this
transform(x) { return x; } ← method syntax
process: function() { return 1; } ← function expression
Accessor Descriptors (have get/set):
Properties: Getters/setters
This Matches Your Test Expectations
Looking at test 8:
typescript
const api = {
    field: 'text',
    process: function() { return 1; },  // method ✓
    compute: () => 2,                   // field (arrow) ✓
    transform(x: number) { return x * 3; }  // method ✓
};
expect(instance.$methods().length).toBe(3);  // Expects 3
Wait - the test expects 3 methods, but with my classification only 2 are methods (process and transform). The arrow compute would be a field.
Let me look at test 8 again... Actually test 8 is "should filter only fields" and expects 2:
typescript
expect(fields.length).toBe(2);  // 'field' and... what else?
If arrows are fields, then we'd have: field (string) and compute (arrow) = 2 fields ✓
And test 10 expects 3 methods... but only process, transform are methods by this definition. Let me check what the third one is in the test...
Actually, looking more carefully at test 10:
typescript
const api = {
    field: 'text',
    process: function() { return 1; },
    compute: () => 2,
    transform(x: number) { return x * 3; }
};
const methods = instance.$methods();
expect(methods.length).toBe(3);
If the test expects 3, but only 2 fit your philosophical distinction, then either the test is wrong, or I'm misunderstanding what you want.
What do YOU want 0 to be classified as? Method or field?

19:19 UTC, 8 November 2025
typescript
protected getName(): string {
        const literal = this[$literal$];
        if (literal === null) return 'null';
        if (literal === undefined) return typeof literal;
        if (this[$roles$].has('type')) return literal.name;
        if (this[$roles$].has('property')) return this.$key!.getName();
        if (this[$roles$].has('identifier')) return this.getSymbol(literal);
        if (typeof literal === "string") return this.getSymbol(literal);
        if (typeof literal === "symbol") return this.getSymbol(literal);
        if (typeofTypes.has(literal)) return literal.name.toLowerCase();
        if (typeof literal === 'function') return literal.name || 'function';
        return this[$role$];
    }

    protected getDescription(): string {
        const literal = this[$literal$];
        if (literal === null) return this.getName();
        if (literal === undefined) return this.getName();
        if (this[$roles$].has('type')) return this.getName();
        if (this[$roles$].has('property')) return this.getName();
        if (typeof literal === "string") return `"${literal}"`;
        if (typeof literal === "symbol") return `${'${'}${literal.description}}`;
        if (typeofTypes.has(literal)) return literal.name.toLowerCase();
        if (typeof literal === 'function') return literal.name ? `${literal.name}()` : `()`;
        if (typeof literal === 'object') return `{properties:${this.$properties.length}}`;
        return literal.toString();
    }

    protected getSymbol(literal: string | symbol) {
        if (typeof literal === "string") return literal;
        if (typeof literal === "symbol") return `${'${'}${literal.description}}`;
        return '';
    }
Why does getName for a property equal "property" for a property? Shouldn't it be the name of the $key?

19:20 UTC, 8 November 2025
FOUND IT! There's a bug in the constructor:
typescript
constructor(role: $ObjectiveRole, of: $ObjectiveRep | $ObjectiveRole, literal: any, lib?: $Library) {
    this.#lib = lib;
    this[$role$] = role;
    this[$literal$] = literal;
    this[$typeof$] = typeof literal;
    this[$of$] = of;
    if (this[$of$] === 'JavaScript')
        this[$canonical$] = this;
    if (lib && this[$canonical$]) {
        let $this = $lib.$find(this[$canonical$]) as $ObjectiveRep;
        if ($this) return $this.$as(role).$of(of as any);
        lib.$index(this);
        this[$roles$].set(role, this);           // ← ADDS role to map
        if (typeof of === 'string')
            this[$roles$].set(this.$role.$ref, this);
    } else {
        this[$canonical$] = this;                // ← DOESN'T add role to map!
    }
}
The Problem
In the else block, you set this[$canonical$] = this but you never add the role to the roles map!
So when you create a property without a library:
typescript

//                                                                  ^^^^^^^^^ no lib
The roles map stays empty, so this[$roles$].has('property') returns false.
Then in getName():
typescript
if (this[$roles$].has('property')) return this.$key!.getName();  // ← Skipped!
// ... falls through ...
return this[$role$];  // ← Returns 'property'
The Fix
typescript
} else {
    this[$canonical$] = this;
    this[$roles$].set(role, this);  // ← ADD THIS
    if (typeof of === 'string')
        this[$roles$].set(this.$role.$ref, this);
}
This ensures the role is always added to the roles map, whether or not there's a library.

Claude
19:33 UTC, 8 November 2025
Found the bugs! There are TWO issues in getProperties():
Bug 1: Wrong Key in Map (Lines 372, 377)
typescript
this[$propertiesMap$]!.set($descriptor.$key!.literal.property, $descriptor[$literal$]);
//                                              ^^^^^^^^^ WRONG!
$descriptor.$key!.literal IS the property name (string/symbol). You're then trying to access .property on a string, which returns undefined.
So you're setting: map.set(undefined, ...) for every property!
The Fix:
typescript
this[$propertiesMap$]!.set($descriptor.$key!.literal, $descriptor);
//                          remove .property ^^^^^^   ^^^^^^^^^^^ also store wrapper not literal
Bug 2: Storing Raw Descriptors (Lines 372, 377)
You're storing $descriptor[$literal$] (the raw PropertyDescriptor) instead of $descriptor (the $ObjectiveRep wrapper).
This is the same bug as before - store the wrapper, not the raw descriptor.
Why $properties Works But $property Doesn't
When you call $properties('all'):
Returns the $properties$ array, which is correctly built by concatenating inherited properties
✅ Works fine
When you call $property('toString', 'all'):
Checks the map: this[$propertiesMap$]!.get('toString')
But the map keys are all undefined (due to .literal.property bug)
So it never finds 'toString' in the map
Falls through to delegation chain
Eventually returns something, but with broken $value access (due to storing raw descriptor)
The Complete Fix for Lines 372 and 377
typescript
// Line 372 (primitives)
this[$propertiesOwn$].forEach($descriptor => 
    this[$propertiesMap$]!.set($descriptor.$key!.literal, $descriptor));

// Line 377 (types)  
this[$propertiesOwn$].forEach($descriptor => 
    this[$propertiesMap$]!.set($descriptor.$key!.literal, $descriptor));
Both need the same fix: remove .property and store $descriptor instead of $descriptor[$literal$].

Found it! The issue is in getProperties() line 383:
typescript
for (const property in descriptors) {  // ← Only iterates STRING keys!
    const descriptor = descriptors[property] as any;
    descriptor.property = property;
    const $property = new $ObjectiveRep('property', $this, descriptor, this[$canonical$].#lib);
    this[$propertiesOwn$].push($property);
    this[$propertiesMap$].set(property, $property);

The Problem
for...in loops only iterate over string keys, never symbol keys!
Even though Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptors() correctly returns symbol properties in its result, the for...in loop skips them.
The Fix
Use Reflect.ownKeys() or a combination approach:
typescript
const descriptors = Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptors(this[$literal$]);
const keys = Reflect.ownKeys(descriptors);  // Gets both strings AND symbols
for (const property of keys) {
    const descriptor = descriptors[property] as any;
    descriptor.property = property;
    const $property = new $ObjectiveRep('property', $this, descriptor, this[$canonical$].#lib);
    this[$propertiesOwn$].push($property);
    this[$propertiesMap$].set(property, $property);
}
Or equivalently:
typescript
for (const property of [...Object.keys(descriptors), ...Object.getOwnPropertySymbols(descriptors)]) {
But Reflect.ownKeys() is cleaner and does exactly what you need - returns all property keys (both strings and symbols) in a single array.

Typescript complains that descriptors doesn't take symbols. Does it?
Claude
19:40 UTC, 8 November 2025
You're right! TypeScript's type for Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptors() is:
typescript
PropertyDescriptorMap // which is { [key: string]: PropertyDescriptor }
It only accepts string indexing in the type definition, even though at runtime it DOES include symbol properties.
The Fix
Use Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor() (singular) for each key:
typescript
this[$propertiesOwn$] = [];
this[$propertiesMap$] = new Map();
const keys = Reflect.ownKeys(this[$literal$]);  // strings + symbols
for (const property of keys) {
    const descriptor = Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor(this[$literal$], property) as any;
    descriptor.property = property;
    const $property = new $ObjectiveRep('property', $this, descriptor, this[$canonical$].#lib);
    this[$propertiesOwn$].push($property);
    this[$propertiesMap$].set(property, $property);
}
Or use a type assertion on the descriptors object:
typescript
const descriptors = Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptors(this[$literal$]) as Record<string | symbol, PropertyDescriptor>;
const keys = Reflect.ownKeys(descriptors);
for (const property of keys) {
    const descriptor = descriptors[property] as any;
    // ... rest of code
}
The first approach (using singular getOwnPropertyDescriptor) is more explicit about what's happening.

19:45 UTC, 8 November 2025
Looking at the screenshot, the $role.$ref shows: $propertyof({properties:0}:${test})
You want it to show the type name instead of the verbose description. For a property of an instance, you want to see the instance's type (like object) rather than its description.
The Fix
typescript
get $role(): { role: string, of: $ObjectiveRep | string, $ref: string } {
    const $this = this;
    const $role = { 
        role: this[$role$], 
        of: this[$of$], 
        get $ref() {
            let $rep = $this[$of$] === 'JavaScript' ? $this.$name :
                typeof $this[$of$] === 'string' ? $this[$of$] :
                $this[$of$][$roles$]?.has('type') ? $this[$of$].$name :
                $this[$of$].$type.$name;
            if ($this.$name && $rep !== $this.$name)
                $rep = `${$rep}:${$this.$name}`;
            return `${$this[$role$]}of(${$rep})`;
        },
        toString: () => $role.$ref
    } as any;
    return $role;
}
The logic:
If "of" is 'JavaScript' → use this.$name (for types like 'string', 'number')
If "of" is a string role → use that string
If "of" is a type → use the type's name directly ($of$.$name)
Otherwise → use the type OF the "of" ($of$.$type.$name)
Result for your test:
Property of instance of Object
Instance is not a type, so use instance.type.type.type.name = 'object'
Property name is ${test}
Final: propertyof(object:${test}) ✓
Much cleaner!

Should this be true? constructor wouldn't be returned as a method
Claude
20:06 UTC, 8 November 2025
You're right to question this! The test expectation is probably wrong.
Why 'constructor' Shouldn't Be a Method
It's metadata, not a callable method: You don't call instance.constructor() as a method - you use it to get the constructor function
Your old commented code explicitly excluded it: ``typescript // if (name === 'constructor' && this.#kinds.has('prototype')) continue;``
Semantic distinction: Methods are functions you call on instances. constructor is the function that CREATES instances.
The Fix
In getProperties(), when enumerating prototype properties (lines ~383-390), filter out 'constructor':
typescript
const keys = Reflect.ownKeys(this[$literal$]);
for (const property of keys) {
    if (property === 'constructor') continue;  // ← Add this
    const descriptor = Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor(this[$literal$], property) as any;
    // ... rest of code
}
But wait - you're getting properties from different branches (primitives, types, instances). Where exactly does the constructor show up?
For types (line 368-377), you call:
typescript
this[$propertiesOwn$] = this.$prototype.getProperties('own', this);
So the filtering should happen when getting properties from a prototype. Looking at the code, the instance branch (lines 378-390) handles regular objects, but when IS something a prototype?
The branch doesn't explicitly check isPrototype(). I think you need to add the constructor filter in the regular enumeration block, but check if we're dealing with a prototype's properties:
typescript
for (const property of keys) {
    // Skip constructor when enumerating prototype properties
    if (property === 'constructor' && this[$literal$].constructor?.prototype === this[$literal$]) {
        continue;
    }
    // ... rest
}
Or simpler: just always skip 'constructor' for types specifically, since types enumerate their prototype's properties.
Update the test to not expect 'constructor':
typescript
expect(names).not.toContain('constructor');  // ← Change expectation

20:42 UTC, 8 November 2025
Okay I have all the tests working. Here's the fun part. We get to make the duck typing constructors
You can see commented out old versions in here
Now, let's come up with them based on $ObjectiveRep
The trick is overloading $as and $is very specifically. $of is not exposed. That is controlled by us
The user would just have access to $instanceof and $typeof, not $type. That's private
And we can define all of the roles
Here are the roles:
typescript
export type $ObjectiveRole = 'object' | 'function' | 'primitive' | 'array' | 'parameter' | 'instance' | 'prototype' | 'type' | 'constructor' | 'class' | 'generic' | 'member' | 'field' | 'property' | 'method' | 'getter' | 'setter' | 'identifier' | 'value' | 'JavaScript';
Here's $as and $is
typescript
$is(type: Type): boolean;
    $is(type: TypeofType): boolean;
    $is(role: $ObjectiveRole): boolean;
    $is(role: $ObjectiveRole | Type | TypeofType): boolean {
        if (role === undefined)
            return this[$literal$] === undefined;
        if (typeof role === 'string')
            return this[$typeof$] === role || this[$roles$].has(role);
        return this.$equals($type(role));
    }

    $as(role: $ObjectiveRole): $ObjectiveRep {
        if (this[$role$] == role) return this;
        if (this[$roles$].has(role))
            return this[$roles$].get(role)!;
        const $this = this.$new(this[$canonical$]);
        $this[$role$] = role;
        $this[$roles$].set(role, $this);
        if (typeof this[$of$] === 'string')
            this[$roles$].set($this.$role.$ref, $this);
        return $this;
    }
You aren't going to use $ObjectiveRole. You are going to manually specify the string. Remember, $ObjectiveRep doesn't implement these interfaces. Total duck typing
But we can guide the user through what is possible
Now can we make typeguards in the interfaces from $is to help with $as?
What are the interfaces you will make?
Here are all the roles, but we don't need to support them all. Just the ones that make sense.
And for some, literal doesn't even make sense! Like properties and parameters. No one needs to see the literals!
typescript
export type $ObjectiveRole = 'object' | 'function' | 'primitive' | 'array' | 'parameter' | 'instance' | 'prototype' | 'type' | 'constructor' | 'class' | 'generic' | 'member' | 'field' | 'property' | 'method' | 'getter' | 'setter' | 'identifier' | 'value' | 'JavaScript';
So what do you think? Don't write the code. But let's enumerate the interfaces and talk about design
Claude
20:43 UTC, 8 November 2025
Interface Design Discussion
Core Philosophy
These interfaces are perspectives, not inheritance hierarchies. The same underlying $ObjectiveRep can be viewed through different lenses via $as. The interfaces guide users on what's valid to access in each perspective.
Proposed Interface Tiers
Tier 1: Essential User-Facing Interfaces
$Instance - What $instanceof() returns
typescript
interface $Instance {
  literal: any;                           // The actual runtime value
  $type: $Type;                          // Navigate to its type
  $prototype: $Prototype;                // Navigate to its prototype
  $property(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property;
  $properties(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property[];
  $field(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Field;
  $fields(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Field[];
  $method(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Method;
  $methods(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Method[];
  
  $is(role: 'primitive'): this is $Primitive;
  $as(role: 'primitive'): $Primitive;
}
$Type - What $typeof() returns or instance.$type
typescript
interface $Type {
  literal: Function;                      // The constructor function
  $prototype: $Prototype;                // The prototype object
  $constructor: $Constructor;            // Itself as constructor
  $property(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property;
  $properties(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property[];
  $methods(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Method[];
  
  $is(role: 'constructor'): this is $Constructor;
  $as(role: 'constructor'): $Constructor;
}
$Property - Generic property view (before specialization)
typescript
interface $Property {
  // NO literal - that's the descriptor internals!
  $name: string;                         // Property name
  $key: { literal: string | symbol };    // Key wrapper
  $value: $Instance;                     // The value (if data descriptor)
  $getter: $Instance;                    // Getter function (if accessor)
  $setter: $Instance;                    // Setter function (if accessor)
  
  isReadable: boolean;
  isWritable: boolean;
  isConfigurable: boolean;
  isEnumerable: boolean;
  isField: boolean;
  isMethod: boolean;
  isProperty: boolean;                   // Has get/set
  
  $is(role: 'field'): this is $Field;
  $is(role: 'method'): this is $Method;
  $as(role: 'field'): $Field;
  $as(role: 'method'): $Method;
}
$Field - Non-function data property
typescript
interface $Field {
  $name: string;
  $value: $Instance;                     // The field's value
  isConfigurable: boolean;
  isEnumerable: boolean;
  isWritable: boolean;
}
$Method - Function data property
typescript
interface $Method {
  $name: string;
  $value: $Instance;                     // The function itself
  $parameters: $Parameter[];
  form: 'method' | 'function';
  isAsync: boolean | undefined;
  isGenerator: boolean | undefined;
  isConfigurable: boolean;
  isEnumerable: boolean;
}
$Parameter - Function parameter
typescript
interface $Parameter {
  // NO literal - just { rest: boolean }!
  isRest: boolean;
}
Tier 2: Useful Secondary Interfaces
$Primitive - Primitive value representation
typescript
interface $Primitive {
  literal: string | number | boolean | symbol | bigint | null | undefined;
  $type: $Type;                          // String, Number, etc.
  // Properties delegated to type
  $properties(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property[];
}
$Prototype - Prototype object
typescript
interface $Prototype {
  literal: object;
  $type: $Type;                          // Usually Object or parent class
  $property(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property;
  $properties(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property[];
  $methods(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Method[];
}
$Constructor - Emphasizes constructability
typescript
interface $Constructor {
  literal: Function;
  $parameters: $Parameter[];             // Constructor parameters
  // Same as $Type otherwise
}
Tier 3: Internal/Future
Don't expose:
'identifier' - internal key wrapper
'value' - internal value wrapper
'JavaScript' - internal marker
'getter'/'setter' - accessed via $property.$getter, not as roles
Key Design Decisions
1. No literal on Property/Parameter
These are wrappers around descriptors and param info. Users don't need to see { value: x, writable: true } or { rest: false }.
2. $is as Type Guards
typescript
if (prop.$is('field')) {
  prop.$value  // TypeScript knows this is valid
}
3. $as with Overloads
typescript
$as(role: 'field'): $Field;
$as(role: 'method'): $Method;
// Not: $as(role: string): any;
4. Minimal Base Interface
typescript
interface $Rep {
  $name: string;
  $ref: string;
  $role: { role: string, of: any, $ref: string };
}
Everything has these, but not everything is the same TYPE.
5. Navigation is One-Way
Instance → Type ✓
Type → Prototype ✓
Property → Field/Method ✓
But NO: Field → Property (you'd lose type safety)
User Journey Examples
Inspect object properties:
typescript
const instance = $instanceof({ x: 1, compute: () => 2 });
for (const prop of instance.$properties()) {
  if (prop.$is('field')) {
    console.log(`Field ${prop.$name}:`, prop.$value.literal);
  } else if (prop.$is('method')) {
    console.log(`Method ${prop.$name}, params:`, prop.$parameters.length);
  }
}
Inspect class methods:
typescript
const type = $typeof(new MyClass());
const methods = type.$methods();  // Already filtered to methods
Check constructor signature:
typescript
const ctor = type.$as('constructor');
console.log('Constructor params:', ctor.$parameters);
Questions for You
Should $Instance and $Type share a common parent? They both have $properties(), $methods(), etc.
Do we need $Function for standalone functions (not methods)? Like $instanceof(Math.sin)?
Should $Property expose both 0/1 predicates AND 2 type guards? Seems redundant but practical.
What about arrays? Special $Array interface or just check Array.isArray(instance.literal)?
Should we hide $role entirely? It's useful for debugging ($ref) but exposes internals.
What do you think?

21:36 UTC, 8 November 2025
No definitely not there. First of all, I think instance is $Object, even if the role is instance. And it would need to be is-bound to all the others with the type guards. You're missing $constructor on there, but that's small, and remember that $is handles undefined as a literal
Anything that expresses $properties would express $property, $methods, $method, $fields, $field - as they can have all of them in principle
And then I REALLY think you need to $is and $as up a lot of options
I don't think a $prototype is a real thing
Also you can make some of them generic and bind the literal right?
There's so much more you can do!!
Claude
21:38 UTC, 8 November 2025
A More Ambitious Interface Design
Core Principle
Everything is $Object, just wearing different hats. The interfaces describe what you can ACCESS in each perspective, with RICH type guards to navigate between them all.
The Main Interfaces
$Object<T = any> - The Universal Entry Point
typescript
interface $Object<T = any> {
  literal: T;
  $name: string;
  $ref: string;
  $type: $Type;
  $prototype: $Object;  // Returns another $Object!
  $constructor: $Constructor;
  
  // The full property suite
  $property(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property;
  $properties(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property[];
  $field(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Field;
  $fields(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Field[];
  $method(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Method;
  $methods(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Method[];
  
  // Rich type guards - can check EVERYTHING
  $is(literal: undefined): boolean;  // Special case
  $is(role: 'primitive'): this is $Primitive;
  $is(role: 'function'): this is $Function;
  $is(role: 'type'): this is $Type;
  $is(role: 'constructor'): this is $Constructor;
  $is(role: 'array'): this is $Array;
  $is(type: typeof String): boolean;  // Can check type constructors
  $is(type: typeof Number): boolean;
  // ... all primitive types
  
  // Rich transformations - can become ANYTHING valid
  $as(role: 'primitive'): $Primitive;
  $as(role: 'function'): $Function;
  $as(role: 'type'): $Type;
  $as(role: 'constructor'): $Constructor;
  $as(role: 'array'): $Array;
  $as(role: 'prototype'): $Object;  // Just returns $Object
}
$Primitive<T extends PrimitiveType = any> - Primitive Values
typescript
interface $Primitive<T extends string | number | boolean | symbol | bigint | null | undefined = any> {
  literal: T;
  $name: string;
  $type: $Type;  // String, Number, Boolean, etc.
  
  // Primitives delegate to type for properties
  $property(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property;
  $properties(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property[];
  $methods(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Method[];
  
  // Can transform back
  $is(role: 'object'): this is $Object<T>;
  $as(role: 'object'): $Object<T>;
}
$Function<T extends Function = Function> - Any Function
typescript
interface $Function<T extends Function = Function> {
  literal: T;
  $name: string;
  $type: $Type;  // Function
  $prototype: $Object;
  $constructor: $Constructor;  // Function constructor
  
  // Functions ARE objects
  $property(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property;
  $properties(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property[];
  $fields(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Field[];
  $methods(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Method[];
  
  // Function-specific
  $parameters: $Parameter[];
  form: 'lambda' | 'function' | 'method' | 'getter' | 'setter' | 'class' | 'unknown';
  isAsync: boolean | undefined;
  isGenerator: boolean | undefined;
  isNative: boolean | undefined;
  hasRest: boolean;
  
  // Can become more specific
  $is(role: 'type'): this is $Type;
  $is(role: 'constructor'): this is $Constructor;
  $is(role: 'method'): boolean;  // Check if it's method form
  $as(role: 'type'): $Type;
  $as(role: 'constructor'): $Constructor;
  $as(role: 'object'): $Object<T>;
}
$Type<T extends Constructor = Constructor> - Constructor/Class
typescript
interface $Type<T extends Constructor = Constructor> {
  literal: T;
  $name: string;
  $prototype: $Object;  // The prototype object
  $constructor: $Constructor;  // Itself as constructor
  $type: $Type;  // Function (its own type)
  
  // Types enumerate their prototype's properties
  $property(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property;
  $properties(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property[];
  $field(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Field;
  $fields(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Field[];
  $method(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Method;
  $methods(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Method[];
  
  // Type-specific
  $parameters: $Parameter[];  // Constructor params
  
  // Can view differently
  $is(role: 'constructor'): this is $Constructor<T>;
  $is(role: 'function'): this is $Function<T>;
  $as(role: 'constructor'): $Constructor<T>;
  $as(role: 'function'): $Function<T>;
  $as(role: 'class'): $Type<T>;  // Same as type
}
$Constructor<T extends Constructor = Constructor> - Emphasizes Construction
typescript
interface $Constructor<T extends Constructor = Constructor> {
  literal: T;
  $name: string;
  $prototype: $Object;
  $type: $Type;
  $constructor: $Constructor;  // Self-reference
  
  // Full suite
  $properties(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property[];
  $methods(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Method[];
  
  // Constructor-specific
  $parameters: $Parameter[];
  
  // Navigate back
  $is(role: 'type'): this is $Type<T>;
  $is(role: 'function'): this is $Function<T>;
  $as(role: 'type'): $Type<T>;
  $as(role: 'function'): $Function<T>;
}
$Array<T = any> - Array Specialization
typescript
interface $Array<T = any> {
  literal: T[];
  $name: string;
  $type: $Type;  // Array
  $constructor: $Constructor;  // Array constructor
  
  // Full property suite (includes numeric indices!)
  $property(name: string | symbol | number, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property;
  $properties(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Property[];
  $fields(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Field[];  // The elements
  $methods(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Method[];  // push, pop, etc.
  
  $is(role: 'object'): this is $Object<T[]>;
  $as(role: 'object'): $Object<T[]>;
}
Property Interfaces
$Property<T = any> - Base Property View
typescript
interface $Property<T = any> {
  $name: string;
  $key: $Object<string | symbol>;  // Identifier wrapper
  
  // Conditionally available based on descriptor type
  $value: $Object<T>;  // Only if data descriptor
  $getter: $Function;  // Only if accessor
  $setter: $Function;  // Only if accessor
  
  // Predicates
  isReadable: boolean;
  isWritable: boolean;
  isConfigurable: boolean;
  isEnumerable: boolean;
  isField: boolean;
  isMethod: boolean;
  isProperty: boolean;  // Has get/set
  
  // Transform to specific views
  $is(role: 'field'): this is $Field<T>;
  $is(role: 'method'): this is $Method;
  $is(role: 'getter'): boolean;
  $is(role: 'setter'): boolean;
  $as(role: 'field'): $Field<T>;
  $as(role: 'method'): $Method;
}
$Field<T = any> - Data Property
typescript
interface $Field<T = any> {
  $name: string;
  $value: $Object<T>;
  isWritable: boolean;
  isConfigurable: boolean;
  isEnumerable: boolean;
  
  $is(role: 'property'): this is $Property<T>;
  $as(role: 'property'): $Property<T>;
}
$Method<T extends Function = Function> - Function Property
typescript
interface $Method<T extends Function = Function> {
  $name: string;
  $value: $Function<T>;
  $parameters: $Parameter[];
  form: 'method' | 'function';
  isAsync: boolean | undefined;
  isGenerator: boolean | undefined;
  isConfigurable: boolean;
  isEnumerable: boolean;
  
  $is(role: 'property'): this is $Property<T>;
  $is(role: 'function'): boolean;
  $as(role: 'property'): $Property<T>;
}
$Parameter - Function Parameter
typescript
interface $Parameter {
  // No literal exposed
  isRest: boolean;
}
Usage Examples
Navigate freely:
typescript
const obj = $instanceof({ x: 1 });

if (obj.$type.$is('type')) {
  const type = obj.$type;  // TypeScript knows it's $Type
  const methods = type.$methods();
}

if (obj.$is('function')) {
  const func = obj;  // TypeScript knows it's $Function
  console.log(func.$parameters);
}
Generic typing:
typescript
const str = $instanceof("hello");  // $Object<string>
if (str.$is('primitive')) {
  const prim = str;  // $Primitive<string>
  const literal: string = prim.literal;  // Type-safe!
}
Property specialization:
typescript
for (const prop of obj.$properties()) {
  if (prop.$is('field')) {
    const field = prop;  // $Field
    console.log(field.$value.literal);
  } else if (prop.$is('method')) {
    const method = prop;  // $Method
    console.log(method.$parameters.length);
  }
}
Questions Back
Should $Object.$prototype return $Object or a special $Prototype interface? I vote $Object.
Do we need distinct interfaces for 'getter' and 'setter' roles, or just access via $property.$getter?
Should there be an $Unknown for things that fail to parse or are truly unknown?
How generic should we go? $Object<T>, $Field<T>, $Method<T extends Function>?
Should $is() support checking multiple roles at once? Like $is('function', 'type')?
This feels more complete - what am I still missing?

Claude
21:41 UTC, 8 November 2025
I'll create a comprehensive interface definition that captures the full art of JavaScript's reflection through rich type guards and transformations.
Created /home/claude/reflection-interfaces.ts
I've created comprehensive interface definitions that capture the full art of JavaScript reflection. Here are the key design elements:
The Art in $is and $as
$is provides rich type narrowing across:
All roles (primitive, function, type, constructor, array, etc.)
JavaScript typeof results ('string', 'number', 'boolean', etc.)
Type constructors (String, Number, Boolean, Object, Array, Function)
Literal undefined check
$as provides fluid transformations between all valid perspectives
Key Design Choices
Everything is $Object: All interfaces flow through $Object as the universal base - you can always view anything as an object
Generics capture literal types: $Object<T>, $Primitive<T>, $Function<T extends Function>, $Array<T> - TypeScript knows what the literal is
Full property suites everywhere: Anything that can have properties exposes the complete API: $property, $properties, $field, $fields, $method, $methods
No internal exposure: Users see literal where meaningful, but not descriptors or parameter objects
Duck typing perfection: $ObjectiveRep doesn't implement these - it satisfies them naturally through its API
The beauty is completeness: Every valid transformation is expressed, capturing JavaScript's fluid, dynamic nature
The interfaces are a contract and a poem - they describe not what objects ARE, but what you can SEE and DO with them from different perspectives. That's the art.

You need $Member as the unspecialized base:
typescript
interface $Member {
  $name: string;
  $key: $Object<string | symbol>;
  
  // Predicates - what IS this?
  isReadable: boolean;
  isWritable: boolean;
  isConfigurable: boolean;
  isEnumerable: boolean;
  isField: boolean;
  isMethod: boolean;
  isProperty: boolean;
  
  // Type guards - specialize me
  $is(role: 'property'): this is $Property;
  $is(role: 'field'): this is $Field;
  $is(role: 'method'): this is $Method;
  $is(role: 'member'): this is $Member;
  
  // Transforms
  $as(role: 'property'): $Property;
  $as(role: 'field'): $Field;
  $as(role: 'method'): $Method;
  $as(role: 'member'): $Member;
}
Then $Property, $Field, $Method extend it:
typescript
interface $Property extends $Member {
  $getter: $Function;
  $setter: $Function;
}

interface $Field<T = any> extends $Member {
  $value: $Object<T>;
}

interface $Method<T extends Function = Function> extends $Member {
  $value: $Function<T>;
  $parameters: $Parameter[];
  form: 'method' | 'function';
  isAsync: boolean | undefined;
  isGenerator: boolean | undefined;
}
And $Object.$property() returns $Member:
typescript
interface $Object<T = any> {
  $property(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Member;  // Not $Property!
  $properties(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Member[];
  
  $field(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Field;  // Pre-specialized
  $fields(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Field[];
  
  $method(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Method;
  $methods(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Method[];
}
This is the ONLY place where inheritance makes sense - because you literally need to discover what a member IS before accessing its specialized features. Elsewhere, everything is roles/perspectives, but here there's a real type hierarchy.

22:32 UTC, 8 November 2025
Okay! Now what are the overloads for these two that help you get as much compile time value out of using them!
typescript
export function $instanceof(literal: any): $ObjectiveRep {
    if (literal === null || literal === undefined)
        return $type(literal).$as('primitive');
    const type = $typeof(literal);
    const of = type[$of$];
    return of as $ObjectiveRep;
}

export function $typeof(literal: any): $ObjectiveRep {
    if (literal === null || literal === undefined)
        return $type(undefined);
    if (literal == Object.prototype) {
        const $object = $type(Object);
        return $object.$of($object.$prototype);
    }
    const $$type = primitives.has(typeof literal) ?
        $type(primitives.get(typeof literal)!) :
        $type(Object.getPrototypeOf(literal)?.constructor);

    const $$instance = new $ObjectiveRep('instance', $$type, literal);
    return $$type.$of($$instance);
}

22:33 UTC, 8 November 2025
Here are the rich overloads that maximize compile-time type information:
typescript
// $instanceof overloads - from most specific to most general
export function $instanceof(literal: null): $Primitive<null>;
export function $instanceof(literal: undefined): $Primitive<undefined>;
export function $instanceof(literal: string): $Primitive<string>;
export function $instanceof(literal: number): $Primitive<number>;
export function $instanceof(literal: boolean): $Primitive<boolean>;
export function $instanceof(literal: symbol): $Primitive<symbol>;
export function $instanceof(literal: bigint): $Primitive<bigint>;
export function $instanceof<T>(literal: T[]): $Array<T>;
export function $instanceof<T extends Function>(literal: T): $Function<T>;
export function $instanceof<T>(literal: T): $Object<T>;

// $typeof overloads - return the type/constructor
export function $typeof(literal: null): $Type<typeof undefined>;
export function $typeof(literal: undefined): $Type<typeof undefined>;
export function $typeof(literal: string): $Type<typeof String>;
export function $typeof(literal: number): $Type<typeof Number>;
export function $typeof(literal: boolean): $Type<typeof Boolean>;
export function $typeof(literal: symbol): $Type<typeof Symbol>;
export function $typeof(literal: bigint): $Type<typeof BigInt>;
export function $typeof<T>(literal: T[]): $Type<typeof Array>;
export function $typeof<T extends Function>(literal: T): $Type<typeof Function>;
export function $typeof(literal: object): $Type;
export function $typeof(literal: any): $Type;
Usage examples showing compile-time benefits:
typescript
const str = $instanceof("hello");
// Type: $Primitive<string>
str.literal  // Type: string

const arr = $instanceof([1, 2, 3]);
// Type: $Array<number>
arr.literal  // Type: number[]

const fn = $instanceof((x: number) => x * 2);
// Type: $Function<(x: number) => number>
fn.literal  // Type: (x: number) => number
fn.$parameters  // Available!

const obj = $instanceof({ x: 1, y: 2 });
// Type: $Object<{ x: number; y: number }>
obj.literal  // Type: { x: number; y: number }

// $typeof captures constructor types
const strType = $typeof("hello");
// Type: $Type<typeof String>
strType.literal  // Type: typeof String (the String constructor)

const arrType = $typeof([1, 2]);
// Type: $Type<typeof Array>
arrType.literal  // Type: typeof Array

const customInstance = new MyClass();
const customType = $typeof(customInstance);
// Type: $Type (can't infer the specific constructor at compile time)
The key insight: primitive overloads MUST come before the generic 0 overloads because TypeScript matches top-to-bottom, and functions/arrays would match the generic object overload otherwise.

Exactly! We need $PrimitiveType for types that don't have real constructors (undefined, null) or where we're representing the primitive type wrapper:
typescript
export interface $PrimitiveType {
  literal: undefined | null | typeof String | typeof Number | typeof Boolean | typeof Symbol | typeof BigInt;
  $name: string;
  $ref: string;
  $role: { role: string, of: any, $ref: string };
  $prototype: $Object;
  
  $property(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Member;
  $properties(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Member[];
  $method(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Method;
  $methods(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $Method[];
  
  $is(literal: undefined): boolean;
  $is(role: 'type'): this is $PrimitiveType;
  $is(type: Type | TypeofType): boolean;
  
  $as(role: 'type'): $PrimitiveType;
}
Then the overloads become:
typescript
export function $typeof(literal: null): $PrimitiveType;
export function $typeof(literal: undefined): $PrimitiveType;
export function $typeof(literal: string): $PrimitiveType;
export function $typeof(literal: number): $PrimitiveType;
export function $typeof(literal: boolean): $PrimitiveType;
export function $typeof(literal: symbol): $PrimitiveType;
export function $typeof(literal: bigint): $PrimitiveType;
export function $typeof<T>(literal: T[]): $Type<typeof Array>;
export function $typeof<T extends Function>(literal: T): $Type<typeof Function>;
export function $typeof(literal: object): $Type;
export function $typeof(literal: any): $Type | $PrimitiveType;
The distinction:
$Type<T extends Constructor> - for constructable types (Object, Array, Function, custom classes)
$PrimitiveType - for primitive type wrappers and non-constructable types (undefined, null, String, Number, Boolean, Symbol, BigInt when viewed as types of primitive values)

  $ref: string;

Done! The key changes:
$Member is the base - returned by $property() and $properties()
$Property, $Field, $Method extend $Member and add specialized features
All three can transform back to $Member via $as('member')
This is the ONLY inheritance in the system - everywhere else is duck-typed roles
The user journey:
typescript
const member = obj.$property('foo');  // $Member
if (member.$is('field')) {
  member.$value  // TypeScript knows this is valid now
}
Perfect minimal design - the inheritance hierarchy exists only where it's semantically necessary (discovering what a property IS before accessing its specialized features).

typescript

export function $instanceof(literal: any): any {
    if (literal === null || literal === undefined)
        return $type(literal).$as('primitive');
    const type = $typeof(literal) as any as $ObjectiveRep;
    const of = type[$of$];
    return of as $ObjectiveRep;
}

export function $typeof(literal: null): $PrimitiveType;
export function $typeof(literal: undefined): $PrimitiveType;

export function $typeof(literal: any): any {
    if (literal === null || literal === undefined)
        return $type(undefined);
    if (literal == Object.prototype) {
        const $object = $type(Object);
        return $object.$of($object.$prototype);
    }
    const $$type = primitives.has(typeof literal) ?
        $type(primitives.get(typeof literal)!) :
        $type(Object.getPrototypeOf(literal)?.constructor);

    const $$instance = new $ObjectiveRep('instance', $$type, literal);
    return $$type.$of($$instance);
}

export function $type(type: Type | TypeofType): $ObjectiveRep {
    return new $ObjectiveRep('type', 'JavaScript', type, $lib);

export function $type(type: typeof Number): $PrimitiveType;
export function $type(type: typeof Boolean): $PrimitiveType;

    return new $ObjectiveRep('type', 'JavaScript', type, $lib);

const $$instanceof: (literal: any) => $ObjectiveRep = $instanceof as any;
const $$typeof: (literal: any) => $ObjectiveRep = $typeof as any;
const $$type: (type: Type | TypeofType) => $ObjectiveRep = $type as any;
Now tests can use:
$type(String) → returns $PrimitiveType (user-facing with type narrowing)
$$type(String) → returns $ObjectiveRep (test-facing without type narrowing)

$$type(MyClass) → returns $ObjectiveRep
This matches the pattern you established for $instanceof and $typeof - the $$ versions bypass TypeScript's type narrowing for internal/test use.

09:04 UTC, 9 November 2025
AH! The issue is CIRCULAR DEPENDENCY!
Your $value getter calls:
typescript
this.isField || this.isMethod
But isMethod calls:
typescript
this.$value.$type.$is('function')
Infinite recursion! 🔄
The fix is to check the descriptor directly without using isMethod/isField:
typescript
get $value(): $ObjectiveRep { 
    const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
    const $of = this[$of$] as $ObjectiveRep;
    
    if (!descriptor || !this.isReadable) 
        return $$typeof(undefined).$as('value').$of(this);
    
    // Check descriptor type directly - no isMethod/isField to avoid circular dependency
    if ('value' in descriptor) {
        // Data descriptor (field or method) - return the value
        return new $ObjectiveRep('value', this, descriptor.value, this[$canonical$].#lib);
    } else {
        // Accessor descriptor (property getter) - bind and call getter
        return new $ObjectiveRep('value', this, descriptor.get!.bind($of[$literal$])(), this[$canonical$].#lib);
    }
}
Your logic was right - you just need to check 'value' in descriptor instead of isField || isMethod to break the cycle.
The distinction:
Data descriptor: has value property (could be field OR method)
Accessor descriptor: has get/set properties
This way $value works without triggering isMethod, which can then safely call $value to check the function type.

Claude
09:11 UTC, 9 November 2025
typescript
$call<T = any>(...args: any[]): T {
    const role = this[$role$];
    
    if (role === 'constructor' || role === 'type' || role === 'class') {
        // Constructor call - use new
        return new this[$literal$](...args) as T;
    } else if (role === 'member' || role === 'method' || role === 'field') {
        // Member call - extract function from descriptor and bind to owner
        const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
        const func = descriptor.value;
        const $of = this[$of$] as $ObjectiveRep;
        return func.call($of[$literal$], ...args) as T;
    } else {
        // Function call - direct invocation
        return this[$literal$](...args) as T;
    }
}
The logic:
Constructor/Type/Class → new literal(...args)
Creates a new instance
Member/Method/Field → descriptor.value.call($of[$literal$], ...args)
Extracts function from the descriptor
Binds this to the owner object ($of[$literal$])
Preserves method context
Function → literal(...args)
Direct call on the function
This way:
typescript
const obj = { x: 1, compute() { return this.x * 2; } };
const method = $instanceof(obj).$method('compute');
method.$call(); // Returns 2, with correct `this` binding

const Type = $type(MyClass);
const instance = Type.$call(); // new MyClass()

typescript
$call<T = any>(...args: any[]): T {
    if (this[$role$] === 'constructor') {
        // Only when viewed AS constructor
        return new this[$literal$](...args) as T;
    } else if (this[$role$] === 'method') {
        // Only when viewed AS method - bind to owner
        const descriptor = this[$literal$] as PropertyDescriptor;
        const func = descriptor.value;
        const $of = this[$of$] as $ObjectiveRep;
        return func.call($of[$literal$], ...args) as T;
    } else if (this[$role$] === 'function') {
        // Only when viewed AS function - direct call
        return this[$literal$](...args) as T;
    }
    
    throw new Error(`Cannot call ${this[$role$]} - use $as() to view as 'constructor', 'method', or 'function'`);
}

YES! Functions have a length property - it's the number of parameters they expect (excluding rest parameters).
typescript
function foo(a, b, c) {}
foo.length // 3

const bar = (x, y, ...rest) => {}
bar.length // 2 (rest doesn't count)

(() => {}).length // 0
It's a read-only property that lives on every function. So when you do:
typescript
const compute = function named(x: number) { return x * 2; };
const instance = $instanceof(compute);
const names = instance.$members('own').map(p => p.$key?.$name);
You'll see:
'length' (parameter count)
'name' (function name)
Plus any custom properties you added
It's part of JavaScript's function metadata - just like name, prototype, and caller. All functions have it by default.

From the Function object itself:
0 - parameter count (excluding rest)
0 - function name (you extract this)
0 - for constructors (you access this)
From your parsing:
0 - lambda/function/method/getter/setter/class/unknown ✓

What you're MISSING:
Parameter NAMES - You parse count/rest, but not the actual names! ``typescript function foo(x, y, ...rest) {} // You could extract: ['x', 'y', 'rest']``
0 - Bound functions have name like "bound functionName" ``typescript const bound = foo.bind(null); bound.name // "bound foo"``
0 - Has .prototype property ``typescript function Foo() {} // has prototype const bar = () => {} // no prototype, can't be constructor``

Doug
09:24 UTC, 9 November 2025
TypeScript doesn't admit the names of the function parameters- I wonder if it's due to minification concerns?
Claude
09:24 UTC, 9 November 2025
YES! Minification is a HUGE reason.
But more fundamentally, it's about TypeScript's structural typing philosophy:
typescript
type Handler = (x: number, y: string) => void;

function foo(a: number, b: string) {} // Valid Handler!
function bar(num: number, str: string) {} // Also valid Handler!
Parameter names aren't part of the type signature - only their positions and types matter. This is by design:
Minification - (userName, isActive) → (a, b) after build
Structural typing - Types match by shape, not names
JavaScript semantics - Runtime doesn't care about names
Flexibility - You can rename params when implementing interfaces
But at runtime? The names ARE there (until minified):
typescript
function compute(value, multiplier) {}
compute.toString() // "function compute(value, multiplier) {}"
Your parsing extracts this! It's runtime metadata, not type-level information. Which is perfect for reflection - you're introspecting the actual JavaScript, not TypeScript's type abstractions.
You could extend your parsing to extract names:
typescript
params: [
  { name: 'value', rest: false },
  { name: 'multiplier', rest: false }
]
But be aware: minification kills this. In production, they become a, b, etc. So parameter names are development-time metadata only.
Still useful for debugging/tooling though!

Claude
22:17 UTC, 10 November 2025
The problem is JavaScript private field semantics!
You're trying to access #$lib on this[$canonical$]:
typescript
new $ObjectiveRep('identifier', this, this[$literal$].property, this[$canonical$].#$lib);
But private fields can only be accessed on 0, not on other instances (even of the same class). this[$canonical$] is a different instance!
Two solutions:
1. Use the parent's #lib field everywhere (simplest)
Remove #$lib from $ObjectiveRep entirely:
typescript
export class $ObjectiveRep<$RepType extends $ObjectiveRep<$RepType> = any> extends $SubjectiveRep<$ObjectiveRep<$RepType>> {
    // Remove: #$lib?: $Library;
    [$literal$]: any;
    // ...
    
    constructor(role: $ObjectiveRole, of: $ObjectiveRep | $ObjectiveRole, literal: any, lib?: $Library) {
        super(role, of, '', lib);
        // Remove: this.#$lib = lib;
        // Parent already set this.#lib = lib
        // ...
    }
}
Then access it as a protected method on the canonical instance:
typescript
new $ObjectiveRep('identifier', this, this[$literal$].property, this.getLib());

// Add to $SubjectiveRep:
protected getLib(): $Library | undefined {
    return this.#lib;
}
2. Use symbol-based privacy (matches your pattern)
Replace #lib with [$lib$] everywhere to match your existing symbol-based pattern. This allows cross-instance access.
Recommendation: Use solution 1 - one private field in the base class, accessed through a protected getter.

        // Remove: this.#$lib = lib;

        // Access parent's private field from within the class
        return (this as any).#lib;

22:27 UTC, 10 November 2025

Claude
22:28 UTC, 10 November 2025
AH! The problem is the PARENT constructor is returning a cached instance, but then the CHILD constructor still tries to set 0 on that already-initialized cached instance!
Here's what happens:
super(role, of, '', lib) is called
Parent constructor finds cached instance and returns it
Control returns to child constructor
Child tries this.#$lib = lib on the ALREADY INITIALIZED cached instance
💥 "Cannot add private member twice"
The fix: Remove caching from the parent constructor. Only child does caching.
typescript
export class $SubjectiveRep<$RepType extends $SubjectiveRep<$RepType> = any>  {
    #lib?: $Library;
    
    constructor(role: string, of: $RepType | string, ref: string, lib?: $Library, customize?: ($this: $SubjectiveRep<$RepType>) => void) {
        this.#lib = lib;
        this[$role$] = role;
        this[$of$] = of;
        this[$ref$] = ref;
        const $this: $RepType = this as any;
        
        // Set canonical but DON'T do caching here
        if (typeof this[$of$] === 'string')
            this[$canonical$] = this as any;
        else
            this[$canonical$] = $this;
            
        this[$roles$].set(role, $this);
        if (typeof of === 'string')
            this[$roles$].set($this.$role.$ref, $this);
            
        if (customize)
            customize(this);
    }
}

export class $ObjectiveRep extends $SubjectiveRep<$ObjectiveRep> {
    #$lib?: $Library;
    
    constructor(role: $ObjectiveRole, of: $ObjectiveRep | $ObjectiveRole, literal: any, lib?: $Library) {
        super(role, of, '', lib);  // No caching in parent now
        
        // Now safe to set child's private field
        this.#$lib = lib;
        this[$literal$] = literal;
        this[$typeof$] = typeof literal;
        
        // Do caching ONLY here in the child
        if (this[$of$] === 'JavaScript' || this[$of$] === 'TypeScript')
            this[$canonical$] = this;
            
        if (lib && this[$canonical$]) {
            let $this = lib.$find(this[$canonical$]) as $ObjectiveRep;
            if ($this) return $this.$as(role).$of(of as any);
            lib.$index(this[$canonical$]);
        }
    }
}
The principle: Parent sets up basic structure, child handles caching. This way private fields are only set once.

Claude
22:29 UTC, 10 November 2025
OH! I see it now.
The problem is $create() uses Object.create() which creates instances WITHOUT calling the constructor. Those instances don't have private fields initialized. Then when you try to access this[$canonical$].#$lib, you're accessing a private field on an object that was never constructed!
Look at your $create method:
typescript
protected $create(version?: $RepType): $RepType {
    version = version || this as any;
    const $this = Object.create(version!) as $RepType;
    return $this;
}
This creates a new object that prototypally inherits from version, but it never runs the constructor, so private fields are never initialized.

typescript

    $member(name: string | symbol, whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $RepType;
    $members(whos?: 'own' | 'all'): $RepType[];
    
    // But these are ABSTRACT/VIRTUAL - each subtype configures them
}

export class $ObjectiveRep extends $SubjectiveRep {
    // Configuration: members = JavaScript object properties
    $members(whos) { return this.getMembers(whos); }
}

export class $UnionType extends $SubjectiveRep {
    // Configuration: members = union constituents
    $members(whos) { return this.unionConstituents; }
}

export class $GenericType extends $SubjectiveRep {
    // Configuration: members = type parameters
    $members(whos) { return this.typeParameters; }
}
The prototype question is perfect:
Objective: $type(Array).$prototype → Array.prototype (actual JavaScript object)
Subjective union: $type("A | B").$prototype → $type("A.$prototype | B.$prototype") (union of prototypes!)
Subjective generic: $type("Array<T>").$prototype → depends on T's binding
Each realm has the SAME API but DIFFERENT SEMANTICS. That's the "different configuration" - same interface, realm-appropriate implementation.

Claude
23:35 UTC, 10 November 2025
OH! You're distinguishing static soundness from dynamic reflection capabilities.
TypeScript compilation (static intersection):
typescript
type U = A | B;
U.prototype  // Only properties in BOTH A.prototype ∩ B.prototype are accessible
Must be sound - can only guarantee what's definitely there.
Reflection (dynamic superposition):
typescript
$type("A | B").$prototype  
  // → $type("A.prototype | B.prototype")
  // Doesn't collapse yet - maintains superposition

$type("A | B").$prototype.$member('foo')
  // → Returns a role-based representation
  // "foo playing role on (A.prototype | B.prototype)"
  // NOT undefined - it's a potential member

// BINDING is where collapse happens:
const instance: A | B = getOne();
$instanceof(instance).$member('foo')
  // → NOW the union collapses to whichever type instance IS
  // Returns real member or undefined
The semantics:
Union type members exist in potential - they're real roles, just not yet bound. Reflection can express "this member MIGHT exist depending on which branch of the union you're in."
When you bind to an actual instance, the superposition collapses - you had string | number, now you have string specifically, so .prototype resolves to String.prototype (not both).
Reflection ≠ Compilation because:
Compilation: Must be sound → intersection (guaranteed)
Reflection: Can be expressive → union (potential) + runtime binding
Your role-based semantics naturally handles this through $of$ - the member plays different roles until binding forces a collapse to objective reality.
Is that it?
